> ## Summary

> Wrote `/Users/michaelhamilton/Documents/newbillofrights/research/orgs/CROSS_CUTTING_ORGS.md` (299 lines), covering 16 organizations.

> **The headline finding: there is nothing here to adopt.** Of 16 orgs surveyed, only 4 have drafted amendment text, and none is usable for our nine articles. The large multi-issue reform orgs — Issue One, Unite America, RepresentUs, Protect Democracy, IRG, Leadership Now, RDI, Future Caucus — have **zero** amendment texts between them. They are statutory and litigation shops. RepresentUs's flagship is explicitly a model *statute*; Leadership Now routes *around* amendments by design (voluntary retirement norms instead of term limits). That's good news for partnership: none is a competitor in the amendment lane, and none carries route risk.

> **The Article V route conflict has a clean resolution.** The field splits on *mechanism*, not goal — and Common Cause says so in its own words: it "fully supports overturning *Citizens United*... We support a constitutional amendment as one path – but oppose an Article V convention as the mechanism." Since the congressional-proposal route (how all 27 amendments were proposed) sits on the *same side* as the ~230-org opposition coalition, **declaring that route explicitly costs us nothing and keeps the field's institutional weight available**. Notably, Burger and Scalia anchor the anti-convention case — so the runaway-convention fear isn't left-coded and can't be messaged away. Recommended standing rule: separate "adopt their text" from "join their campaign," so USTL's term-limits language can be taken on the merits without joining its convention drive.

> **The ⚖️ verification came back fully confirming COFOUNDER_SEARCH.md.** Treas. Reg. §56.4911-2(d)(1)(i) names "constitutional amendment" in the regulation text itself. Two refinements beyond what the memo claimed: the nonpartisan-analysis exception (§4911(d)(2)) covers the current phase, *but* §56.4911-2(c)(1)(iii) converts research into lobbying if "timed or channeled" to a vote — publishing on our own schedule matters legally, not just reputationally. I recommend the **§501(h) election at formation**: it stops volunteer hours from counting and downgrades the penalty from possible loss of exemption to a 25% tax on the excess.

> **American Promise's structure is confirmed and is the field standard** — c3 Education Fund (47-4601462, Nov 2015) and c4 (47-4608840, Feb 2016), with the c4 now carrying the larger budget. Eight of the surveyed orgs run the same pair; the two exceptions (Leadership Now c4-only, RDI c3-only) confirm the rule that structure follows strategy.

> Three items worth flagging for the founder:
> - **Keep Nine circulates two different texts** — its site says "nine Justices," but H.J.Res.1 as reported says "nine justices consisting of one chief justice and eight associate justices." Quote the joint resolution. It had exactly 3 sponsors, all Republican, and no nonprofit entity exists (free Google Sites page).
> - **The renewdemocracy.org warning resolved**: real, distinct from Kasparov's RDI, has 6 provisions of drafted text — but "nonprofit status is applied for," no named leadership, no entity. Not citable as a peer org.
> - **FairVote independently confirms our proportional-representation blocker**: the best-funded org in that space pursues a *statute*, agreeing by revealed preference that §5.3 has no answer.

> Caveats: all state counts come from advocates or opponents (no official Article V register exists); employee counts were unobtainable (ProPublica returns null and its filing pages 404); and Article V positions for the eight multi-issue orgs are an *unverified absence* — the coalition letter's signer list is six years stale, so those should be asked directly before any coalition commitment.

The landscape splits into two groups, neither of which is the thing the direction contemplates:

> 1. **Deep-expertise orgs that deliberately declined to draft an amendment.** Protect Democracy, CLC, CREW, and Brennan all frame their work as things Congress can do *without* amending. CLC says it outright: solutions "Congress could pursue without a constitutional amendment." Protect Democracy's flagship *Checking the Pardon Power* (Apr. 2024, Tudor &amp; Florence) argues the power is *already* limited and its strongest congressional ask is a **non-binding Sense of Congress resolution**. Their institutional position is the opposite of ours.
> 2. **Members of Congress with text and no org behind it.** Cohen (5 introductions, 2017–2025) and Olszewski (H.J.Res.135). No hearing, no markup, ever. Advocacy groups supply endorsement quotes for press releases; none runs a ratification campaign.

> ## Things worth Michael's attention

> - **The Protect Democracy contradiction.** Grant Tudor gave a supportive quote for Cohen's amendment while the org's own unwithdrawn paper recommends against needing one. A partnership built on that quote would be building on a press release, not a program. Also a concentration risk: we'd be leaning on their polling, framing, and coalition simultaneously.
> - **Kentucky SB 10 is the find of this pass.** A live Nov. 3, 2026 ballot measure requiring clemency "reasons... always open to public inspection" — our Section 1's core — passed 36-0 Senate / 82-12 House (Republicans 73-2), Republican-sponsored, no organized opposition. It updates our red-state argument from 1939–1943 constitutions to the present tense. Pending, so re-check after the election.
> - **Cato is an adversary that validates our design.** Their Feb. 2026 piece attacks the congressional veto for adding a second decision-maker, delay, and politicized review. Our article has none of the three — every argument lands on Olszewski's text and glances off ours. Read it as a road test.
> - **Corpus correction.** `PARDON_RESEARCH.md` says Bacon plus "six further Democratic cosponsors." Live congress.gov shows **seven cosponsors total — Bacon is the one original and the one Republican**. Substance right, count to state publicly is seven. (The govinfo bulk XML doesn't surface Bacon in the same field pattern; congress.gov is the authority.)
> - **Drafting convergence on §3.** Cohen abandoned an enumerated-kinship list in 2021 for "third degree relation" — the same solution as ours. > - **Blumenthal's amendment does not exist.** Announced Jan. 2025, never introduced — verified by a congress.gov SJRES+"pardon" search returning zero results for the 119th.

> **The headline finding: there is nothing to adopt.** No organization has drafted constitutional amendment text on lobbying or the revolving door — verified against all five seed orgs, the Article V groups, and the adversary side. Every actor pursues model statutes, federal bills, or litigation. This makes Article 5 structurally different from campaign finance (American Promise) and term limits (U.S. Term Limits), where the founder direction has real purchase.

> **The AACA benchmark, verbatim from the source PDF.** Provision 3 sets five years for former Members and senior congressional staff — the same number as our Section 1, but congressional-only, with no executive-branch reach. Provision 13's shadow-lobbying trigger ("providing strategic advice to lobbying efforts or directing or supervising the provision of strategic advice") converges with our "advising others in advocating before" and names the supervisory tier more explicitly than we do. Critically, **the AACA contains no foreign-lobbying provision of any kind** — our Section 2, the plank where Republicans lead at 70.5%, has no counterpart in the field's flagship model law. Nor does any org's text contain a citizen-petitioner firewall.

> The AACA is also self-described as "an aspirational document" drafted "with a sharp eye toward avoiding provisions that could be overturned by the courts" — engineered to dodge the First Amendment collision that Article 5 exists to settle. Its statutory precision (20%-vs-12-hour thresholds, 75%-of-salary tiers, named agencies) is exactly the over-specification the memo's counter-trap warns against.

> **Two findings that strengthen the "why constitutional" case.** South Dakota Measure 22 — the only statewide AACA, with a two-year revolving-door ban — passed 51.63% to 48.37%, was enjoined within a month (Dec 8, 2016) and repealed outright by HB 1069 on Feb 2, 2017. Alongside *Miller v. Ziegler* and *Garcia v. Stillman*, the field has now run the statutory experiment for a decade and keeps losing what it wins. Separately, Public Citizen calls a lifetime ban "politically impractical and constitutionally suspect" — a quotable line an opponent will aim at our Section 2, worth anticipating.

> Two caveats worth your attention: the AACA provisions document is dated December 2019 and **anticorruptionact.org, the domain in its own footer, is unreachable** (timeouts, not 403s) — treat it as a legacy platform, not a live drafting partner. And POGO, the deepest FARA expert, proposes only fines and disclosure where we impose a lifetime bar; cite their evidence, not their endorsement.

> Four findings worth flagging beyond the JSON:

> **The task brief had one error I corrected.** New Hampshire's 2018 privacy amendment was **Question 2**, not Question 1.

> **The strongest single discovery is New Hampshire's drafting history.** CACR 16 as introduced read "governmental *interference* in private or personal *matters* is *fundamental*." House Amendment 0118h changed it to "governmental *intrusion* in private or personal *information* is *natural, essential, and inherent*." That is precisely the narrowing our memo's decisional-autonomy trap note prescribes, made independently by a legislature on a public record. And the trap still fired — Cornerstone Action PAC campaigned against it as a stealth abortion measure — yet it passed with ~81%. That is the best available measure of what the abortion shadow actually costs an informational-privacy framing, and it is far less than the framing risk alone implies.

> **Michigan's numbers are stronger than the memo recorded.** SJR G passed the Senate 38–0 (22 R, 16 D) and the House 106–0 — unanimous against a two-thirds requirement — before winning 88.75% at the ballot. The Senate Fiscal Agency also concluded on the record that the amendment "would have a negligible fiscal impact on State and local law enforcement agencies, as it is current practice that agencies seek search warrants." That is a neutral legislative analyst answering the administrative-disruption objection directly.

> **Missouri's coalition is an unusually clean full-spectrum artifact:** the ACLU of Missouri and ALEC were both named supporters of the same ratified constitutional amendment, alongside OffNow and the Constitution Party. Combined with the 50-signatory FANFSA letter (Americans for Prosperity, FreedomWorks, and R Street signing beside the ACLU, Color of Change, and United We Dream), the cross-partisan floor under our §2 rests on organizational evidence independent of the polling.</result>

> **Three findings that change the adoption decision:**

> 1. **American Promise's text exists in two materially different versions.** The website version puts the artificial-entity power in §3 (enforcement, ungoverned by any reasonableness standard) and says "ballot measures"; H.J.Res. 191 as introduced puts it in §2 governed by "reasonably" and says "State or local ballot initiatives or referendums." Taking these in order — #2 is a build task I'll execute now; #1 and #4 get straight answers.

**#1 — you're right, and I overstated it.** Your method's revisability rule is evidence-triggered, and I framed the four ratifications as if they were a trigger. They're admissible evidence (certified ballot results rank high in SELECTION_METHOD's hierarchy), but ballot totals don't give you the thing the gate actually needs — the party floor. Utah passing at 50.3% in a deep-red state *hints* at a Republican near-majority; Ohio failing at 46% cuts the other way. That's exactly your second category: evidence that re-polling is warranted, not evidence that passes the test. Disposition: **stays held; the intent-standard question goes to the commissioned poll.** Let me verify it's in the spec:

Privacy swap is **live** (deploy exit 0, both ratified phrases verified on the page). Now the two answers you asked for:

**#3** — noted for your calendar. If it helps, I can draft the two founder-to-org notes (short, voice-guide-clean, stating what we adopted, where it's credited, and the text-not-campaign distinction) for you to edit and send.

**#4 — the confirmation-refusal default, explained properly.**

The problem every drafter of Supreme Court term limits must solve: with staggered 18-year terms, a vacancy arrives on schedule every two years — so "the Senate simply refuses to act on any nominee" (the Garland scenario, from either party's Senate against either party's president) stops being a freak event and becomes a recurring, exploitable move. The text has to say what happens when the Senate does nothing.

**The field's majority answer — deemed confirmation:** if the Senate doesn't vote within N days, the nominee is *automatically confirmed*. This is what the NCC's cross-ideological drafting teams chose and what the Khanna/Fix the Court bills chose. Its virtue: the Court is always full, and the Senate can't obstruct its way to an eight-member bench. Its cost: **a person can join the Supreme Court without any affirmative Senate consent, ever.** A president whose allies simply run out the clock gets a justice seated with zero votes. That inverts advice-and-consent, and it hands a structural gift to whichever party holds the White House — the exact regime-coded asymmetry your veil-of-ignorance rule exists to forbid. The attack ad writes itself, and it lands with the out-party every time: "this lets the president install judges without a vote."

**Your answer — the knock-out default:** the seat's 18-year clock runs whether or not it's filled, and until it's filled "the Supreme Court shall exercise its judicial power as then constituted." Senate refusal produces… nothing for anyone. No automatic justice for the president; no preserved bench for the obstructors — just a temporarily smaller Court (4–4 splits accepted, affirming the lower court). Nobody profits from blocking, so both sides' cheapest move is to process nominees. And the Senate's consent power is never bypassed — no justice ever sits without a vote.

**So why is it the minority position?** Because the other drafters optimized for a different value: *guaranteed full bench* over *symmetric incentives*. That's a legitimate choice, not evidence you're wrong. The honest cost of yours is the mirror image of theirs: a Senate determined to stonewall a president for years produces an extended eight- (in theory seven-) member Court, and prolonged 4–4s mean circuit splits stand unresolved. Their cost is seating unconsented justices; your cost is a thinner Court during standoffs.

**Why I think your minority position clears your own bar:** deemed confirmation fails the two tests this project treats as constitutive — it isn't symmetric (it always advantages the presidency's current holder), and it removes a chamber's constitutional consent rather than disciplining it. Yours takes a weapon from both sides at once, which is the design signature of your other eight articles. It also polls-proofs better in structure: "no one gains from obstruction" is explainable across the spectrum; "auto-confirmation after 90 days" is a one-line attack.

**And consistent with your rule from #1:** this is exactly what the queued forward-looking appointment-default poll question exists to arbitrate. If the split-sample shows deemed-confirmation reads acceptably to both parties and the knock-out reads badly, that's your revisit trigger. Until the evidence says otherwise, I'd keep your text — with the page annotation saying plainly (as the memo now records) that this is a deliberate departure from the other drafters' choice, and why.

**Standing state after this exchange:** #1 held (with the noted gap — anti-favoritism clause has no poll-question instrument yet; one word from you adds it) · #2 done and live (3f24f3a) · #3 on your calendar (offer to draft the notes stands) · #4 keep, pending the poll evidence.

The Justice longest in continuous service shall serve as Chief Justice, without further appointment."* Drafted as our own words implementing their §§8/7 ideas (their §7 text would have rewritten more of Article II than this article otherwise touches). **Gemini pass — run, verified, applied.** Six findings; **4 applied** (the "word for word" claim now carries the one-added-sentence caveat consistently; the CF "courts are left" open question narrowed — FOFA's text expressly settles ballot measures for §§2–3, so only §4's reach remains open; the fourteen-states count hedged as USTL's own count, matching your memo's advocacy-sourced caution; "red-state" shorthand replaced). **2 rejected as reviewer errors**, documented in the commit: the deliberative-polling sentence follows the existing footnote convention your earlier passes accepted, and Gemini's "2026 is factually impossible" objection is its own knowledge-cutoff confusion — KY SB 10's Nov 3, 2026 date and the Virginia bill are verified correct.

**2) Pardon enforcement clause — your question answered:**

**Was it excluded on purpose?** Semi-deliberately. No dated decision says "no enforcement clause," but the absence fits a documented design: Article 8 is built as *self-executing conditions* — an unpublished pardon "shall not take effect," a banned pardon is void, courts enforce directly, no congressional role. That design is why Cato's attack on Olszewski's congressional-veto text "glances off ours," and it's consistent with your rejection of the congressional-disapproval clause. The memo's implementation section documents the absence and confirms §§1–4 bind without any statute.

**Pros of adding one:** removes any doubt that Congress may legislate the machinery (publication vehicle, deadlines, reporting format, penalties for concealment) against a President claiming exclusive Article II control of clemency mechanics; matches the Reconstruction pattern four of your nine articles use; Cohen's own drafting evolution added one — independent convergence.

**Cons:** it re-opens the flank the article deliberately closed — a general "power to enforce" gives Congress standing authority over the one core mercy power, and hostile Congresses could pile "appropriate legislation" into functional obstruction of clemency; courts sometimes read enforcement clauses as inviting *Boerne*-style fights over what legislation is congruent, and the interaction with the article's "shall not take effect"/void structure is exactly what the memo flags as unanalyzed; and your mission text promises a savings clause protecting *mercy* — a broad enforcement power sits in tension with it.

**Does it need to pass polling?** No — and this is the answer to your procedural instinct: enforcement clauses are machinery, not principle. Your method polls principles and delegates machinery; nobody has ever polled an enforcement clause and no meaningful question could be asked (same reasoning as the uniformity clause's "unpollable in a form that would carry a verdict"). So it's a drafting-counsel decision, not a gate question.

**My recommendation:** don't add a general enforcement clause — the self-executing design is the article's distinctive strength. If you want the certainty benefit without the flank, the narrow form does it: *"Congress may by law provide for the manner of publication and reporting under this article."* Machinery-only, no general power over clemency, preserves the no-second-decision-maker design. Your call — say the word for either version, or leave it as is.
